# Women's National Basketball Association

"WNBA" redirects here. For other uses, see [WNBA (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_\(disambiguation\)).

Not to be confused with the [National Women's Basketball Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Women's_Basketball_Association), a 1986 league.

The **Women's National Basketball Association** (**WNBA**) is a women's professional [basketball](3921.md) league in [North America](21139.md) composed of 15 teams (14 in the [United States](3434750.md) and 1 in [Canada](5042916.md)), scheduled to expand to 18 by 2030. The WNBA is one of the [professional women's sports leagues in North America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prominent_women's_sports_leagues_in_the_United_States_and_Canada#Professional_women's_leagues). The league is headquartered in [New York City](645042.md).[^cite_note-2]

The WNBA was founded on April 24, 1996, as the women's counterpart to the [National Basketball Association](22093.md) (NBA); league play began in 1997.[^cite_note-3] The regular season runs from May to September, with each team playing 44 games. The top eight teams (regardless of conference) qualify for the [playoffs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_playoffs), culminating in the [WNBA Finals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_Finals), which is played in November.

The [All-Star Game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_All-Star_Game) occurs in late-July. The league hosts an annual mid-season competition, the [Commissioner's Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_Commissioner's_Cup). The WNBA is an active member of [USA Basketball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USA_Basketball) (USAB),[^cite_note-4] which is recognized by the [International Basketball Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Basketball_Federation) (FIBA) as the [governing body](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_governing_body) for basketball in the U.S.

## History

### League founded and play begins (1996–1997)



[Sheryl Swoopes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheryl_Swoopes), the first player signed by the WNBA, won four championships with the [Houston Comets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Comets) and three MVP awards.

The creation of the WNBA was officially approved by the NBA Board of Governors on April 24, 1996,[^cite_note-5] and announced at a press conference with players [Rebecca Lobo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rebecca_Lobo), [Lisa Leslie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lisa_Leslie), and [Sheryl Swoopes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheryl_Swoopes) in attendance.[^cite_note-6] The new WNBA had to compete with the recently formed [American Basketball League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Basketball_League_\(1996%E2%80%931998\)) (ABL), another professional women's league that began play in the fall of 1996, but ceased operation during its 1998–99 season.

The WNBA began with eight teams: the [Charlotte Sting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlotte_Sting), [Cleveland Rockers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleveland_Rockers), [Houston Comets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Comets), and [New York Liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Liberty) in the Eastern Conference; and the [Los Angeles Sparks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_Sparks), [Phoenix Mercury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenix_Mercury), [Sacramento Monarchs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacramento_Monarchs), and [Utah Starzz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utah_Starzz) in the Western Conference.[^cite_note-\:0-7]

While the first major women's professional basketball league in the U.S. was the defunct [WBL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_Pro_Basketball_League), the WNBA is the only league to receive full backing of the [NBA](22093.md).[^cite_note-8] The WNBA logo, "Logo Woman", paralleled the NBA logo and was selected out of 50 different designs.[^cite_note-\:0-7]

After a much-publicized gold medal run by the USA women's national team at the [1996 Summer Olympic Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basketball_at_the_1996_Summer_Olympics), the WNBA began its first season on June 21, 1997. The first WNBA game featured the New York Liberty facing the Los Angeles Sparks in Los Angeles. The Liberty defeated the Sparks 67–57. A crowd of 14,284 attended the game at the [Great Western Forum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Western_Forum) in [Inglewood, California](107640.md).[^cite_note-9] The game was televised nationally in the U.S. on the [NBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NBC) television network. At the start of the 1997 season, the WNBA had TV deals with NBC (NBA rights holder), [ESPN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ESPN), and [Lifetime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lifetime_\(TV_channel\)). [Penny Toler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penny_Toler) scored the league's first point.[^cite_note-\:12-10]

### Houston domination and league expansion (1997–2000)



[Cynthia Cooper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cynthia_Cooper-Dyke) entered the WNBA at age 34 and won Finals MVP in each of the Houston Comets' four championships.

The WNBA centered its marketing campaign, dubbed "We Got Next", around stars [Rebecca Lobo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rebecca_Lobo), [Lisa Leslie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lisa_Leslie), and [Sheryl Swoopes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheryl_Swoopes).[^cite_note-11] In the league's first season, Leslie's [Los Angeles Sparks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_Sparks) underperformed, and Swoopes sat out much of the season due to her pregnancy. Perhaps the WNBA's first star was [MVP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_Most_Valuable_Player_Award) [Cynthia Cooper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cynthia_Cooper-Dyke), Swoopes' teammate on the [Houston Comets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Comets). The Comets defeated Lobo's [New York Liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Liberty) in the [first WNBA championship game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_WNBA_Championship).[^cite_note-12] The initial "We Got Next" advertisement ran before each season until it was replaced with a "We Got Game" campaign.

Two teams were added in 1998 (the [Detroit Shock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_Shock) and [Washington Mystics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Mystics)),[^cite_note-13] and two more in 1999 (the [Orlando Miracle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orlando_Miracle) and [Minnesota Lynx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Lynx)), bringing the total number of teams in the league to 12.[^cite_note-14] The 1999 season began with a collective bargaining agreement between players and the league, marking the first [collective bargaining](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collective_bargaining) agreement to be signed in the history of [women's professional sports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_professional_sports).[^cite_note-15] That year, the WNBA also announced it would add four more teams for the 2000 season (the [Indiana Fever](240214.md), [Seattle Storm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Storm), [Miami Sol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami_Sol), and [Portland Fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portland_Fire_\(2000%E2%80%932002\))), bringing the league to 16 teams. WNBA president [Val Ackerman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Val_Ackerman) discussed expansion, saying: "This won't be the end of it. We expect to keep growing the league."[^cite_note-\:25-16]

In 1999, the league's chief competition, the [American Basketball League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Basketball_League_\(1996%E2%80%931998\)) (ABL), declared bankruptcy.[^cite_note-17] Many of the ABL's star players, including several Olympic gold medalists (such as [Nikki McCray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikki_McCray) and [Dawn Staley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dawn_Staley)) and a number of standout college performers (including [Kate Starbird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kate_Starbird) and [Jennifer Rizzotti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jennifer_Rizzotti)), joined the rosters of WNBA teams, enhancing the overall quality of play in the league.[^cite_note-18]

On May 23, 2000, the Comets became the first women's professional team to be invited to the [White House Rose Garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_House_Rose_Garden).[^cite_note-\:11-19][^cite_note-20] At the end of the 2000 season, the Comets won their fourth championship, capturing every title since the league's inception. Led by the "Big Three" of Cooper (who won the [Finals MVP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_Finals_Most_Valuable_Player_Award) for all four championships), Swoopes, and [Tina Thompson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tina_Thompson), the Comets dominated every team in the league.[^cite_note-\:11-19][^cite_note-Lieberman-2003-21] Under head coach [Van Chancellor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Van_Chancellor), the team posted a 98–24 record their first four seasons (16–3 in the playoffs). After 2000, Cooper retired from the league, and the Comets' dynasty came to an end.[^cite_note-Lieberman-2003-21]

### L.A. Sparks success; new league ownership and contraction (2001–2002)



[Lisa Leslie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lisa_Leslie), a three-time MVP and the first player to dunk in a WNBA game

The Los Angeles Sparks had the best record during the [2001 WNBA season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2001_WNBA_season).[^cite_note-22] Led by Lisa Leslie, the Sparks posted a regular-season record of 28–4 and advanced to their first [WNBA Finals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_Finals), sweeping the [Charlotte Sting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlotte_Sting).[^cite_note-23]

Looking for a repeat in 2002, the Sparks again made a strong run toward the postseason, going 25–7 in the regular season under head coach [Michael Cooper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Cooper). Again, Leslie dominated throughout the Playoffs, leading the Sparks to a perfect 6–0 record, beating the New York Liberty in the [2002 Finals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_WNBA_Finals).[^cite_note-24]

Teams and the league were collectively owned by the NBA until the end of 2002,[^cite_note-25] when the NBA sold WNBA teams either to their NBA counterparts in the same city or to a third party as a result of the [dot-com bubble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dot-com_bubble). This led to two teams moving: Utah moved to [San Antonio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Antonio_Stars),[^cite_note-26] and Orlando moved to [Connecticut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Connecticut_Sun)[^cite_note-27] and became the first WNBA team to be owned by a third party instead of an NBA franchise. This sale of teams also led to two teams folding, the Miami Sol and Portland Fire, because new owners could not be found.[^cite_note-28][^cite_note-29][^cite_note-30]

### Bill Laimbeer leaves his mark (2003–2006)



[Lauren Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lauren_Jackson), a three-time MVP and the most accomplished international player in league history

The [Women's National Basketball Players Association](68077461.md) (WNBPA) threatened to strike in 2003 if a new deal was not reached between players and the league. The result was a delay in the start of the 2003 preseason and the [2003 WNBA draft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_WNBA_draft), along with negative publicity for the league.[^cite_note-31]

[Bill Laimbeer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Laimbeer) took over the [Detroit Shock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_Shock) in 2002 as head coach and general manager. He had high hopes for the Shock, despite the team having gone 9–23 its previous season. Three Shock members made it to the [2003 All-Star Game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_WNBA_All-Star_Game) ([Swin Cash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swin_Cash), [Cheryl Ford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheryl_Ford), and [Deanna Nolan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deanna_Nolan)), and Laimbeer orchestrated a worst-to-first turnaround with the Shock finishing the season 25–9 and in first place in the [Eastern Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Conference_\(WNBA\)). In the [2003 Finals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_WNBA_Finals), the Shock defeated the Los Angeles Sparks 2–1, winning game three in front of a league-record crowd of 22,076.[^cite_note-\:14-32][^cite_note-33]

After the [2003 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_WNBA_season), the [Cleveland Rockers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleveland_Rockers), one of the league's original eight teams, folded because its owners were unwilling to continue operating the franchise.[^cite_note-34]



[Sue Bird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sue_Bird) spent her entire WNBA career with the [Seattle Storm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Storm) and is the only player to win titles in three different decades.

The [Seattle Storm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Storm) had consecutive first-overall draft picks in 2001 and 2002, selecting [Lauren Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lauren_Jackson) and [Sue Bird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sue_Bird). The Storm's young core broke through in 2004, finishing second in the [Western Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Conference_\(WNBA\)) and advancing to the [Finals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_WNBA_Finals). In a battle of two first-time finalists, the Storm defeated the [Connecticut Sun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Connecticut_Sun) 2–1 and won their first WNBA title. [Anne Donovan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anne_Donovan) became the first female coach to guide a team to a WNBA championship.[^cite_note-35]

Val Ackerman, the first WNBA president, resigned effective February 1, 2005, citing the desire to spend more time with her family. Ackerman later became president of [USA Basketball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USA_Basketball). On February 15, 2005, NBA commissioner [David Stern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Stern) announced that [Donna Orender](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donna_Orender), who had been serving as the senior vice president of the [PGA Tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PGA_Tour) and played for several teams in the now-defunct [Women's Pro Basketball League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_Pro_Basketball_League), would be Ackerman's successor as of April 2005.[^cite_note-36]

The WNBA awarded an expansion team to Chicago (later named the [Chicago Sky](2717051.md)) in February 2005.[^cite_note-37][^cite_note-38] In the off-season, a set of rule changes was approved that made the WNBA more like the NBA.[^cite_note-39][^cite_note-40]

The [2005 Finals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2005_WNBA_Finals) was the first played in a best-of-five format. In another matchup between two teams seeking their first championship, the Sun came up short for the second consecutive year, falling in four games to the [Sacramento Monarchs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacramento_Monarchs) led by [Yolanda Griffith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yolanda_Griffith).[^cite_note-41]

In 2006, the league reached a milestone as the first team-oriented women's professional sports league to exist for ten consecutive seasons.[^cite_note-\:12-10] On its tenth anniversary, the WNBA released its [All-Decade Team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA's_All-Decade_Team), comprising the ten WNBA players who had contributed, through on-court play and off-court activities, the most to women's basketball during the league's existence.[^cite_note-42]

The Shock bounced back in 2006 behind newly acquired [Katie Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katie_Smith), along with six remaining members from their 2003 championship run. The Shock finished second in the Eastern Conference and knocked out first-seeded Connecticut in the second round of the Playoffs. In the [Finals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_WNBA_Finals), they faced reigning champion Sacramento, winning in Game Five on their home floor.[^cite_note-43]

### Bringing "Paul Ball" to the WNBA (2007–2009)



[Diana Taurasi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diana_Taurasi), the WNBA's all-time leading scorer, spent her entire 20-year WNBA career with the [Phoenix Mercury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenix_Mercury).

In December 2006, the [Charlotte Bobcats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlotte_Hornets) organization announced it would no longer operate the [Charlotte Sting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlotte_Sting). Soon after, the WNBA announced the Sting would not operate for 2007.[^cite_note-44] A [dispersal draft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_WNBA_dispersal_draft) was held on January 8, 2007. Teams selected in inverse order of their 2006 records with the Chicago Sky receiving the first pick.[^cite_note-45]

The [Phoenix Mercury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenix_Mercury) hired coach [Paul Westhead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Westhead) before the 2006 season. His up-tempo style of play was perfect for the team, especially after the league shortened the shot clock from 30 to 24 seconds in 2006. Led by their "Big Three" of [Cappie Pondexter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cappie_Pondexter), [Diana Taurasi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diana_Taurasi), and [Penny Taylor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penny_Taylor), the Mercury averaged a league-record 89.0 points per game in 2007, far surpassing the previous record, and were propelled into first place in the Western Conference.[^cite_note-46] Facing reigning champions the Detroit Shock in the [2007 Finals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_WNBA_Finals), the Mercury beat Detroit on their home floor in front of 22,076 fans in Game Five to claim their first-ever WNBA title.[^cite_note-\:14-32][^cite_note-47]

In October 2007, the WNBA awarded another expansion franchise to Atlanta.[^cite_note-48][^cite_note-49] Atlanta businessman [Ron Terwilliger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ron_Terwilliger) was the original owner of the new team. Citizens of Atlanta voted on the new team's nickname and colors, selecting the name [Atlanta Dream](13930487.md).[^cite_note-DreamName-50]

During the 2008 regular season, the first-ever outdoor professional basketball game in North America, the [Liberty Outdoor Classic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberty_Outdoor_Classic), was played at [Arthur Ashe Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Ashe_Stadium) in New York City. The [Indiana Fever](240214.md) defeated the [New York Liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Liberty) 71–55 in front of over 19,000 fans.[^cite_note-51][^cite_note-52] In the [2008 Finals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_WNBA_Finals), the Shock swept the first-time finalists, the [San Antonio Silver Stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Antonio_Silver_Stars), winning their third championship in six years under coach Bill Laimbeer.[^cite_note-53] The victory effectively marked the end of the Shock dynasty, as Laimbeer resigned early in the 2009 season, with the team relocating shortly thereafter.[^cite_note-54]



[Tamika Catchings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamika_Catchings), a five-time Defensive Player of the Year, spent her entire WNBA career with the [Indiana Fever](240214.md).

Late in 2008, the WNBA took over ownership of one of the league's original franchises, the Houston Comets. The Comets ceased operations on December 1, 2008, after no owners could be found.[^cite_note-55][^cite_note-56] A [dispersal draft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_WNBA_dispersal_draft) took place on December 8, 2008, with the first pick, [Sancho Lyttle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sancho_Lyttle), taken by the Atlanta Dream.[^cite_note-57][^cite_note-58]

Paul Westhead left the Mercury following the 2007 title, and the team faltered in 2008, becoming the first defending champion in league history to miss the playoffs. New head coach [Corey Gaines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corey_Gaines) implemented Westhead's style of play, and in 2009, the Mercury once again broke their own record, averaging 92.8 points per game in the regular season. Helped by the return of Taylor, who missed the 2008 season and a large part of the 2009 season, the Mercury clinched first place in the Western Conference and advanced to the [2009 Finals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_WNBA_Finals).[^cite_note-59] The championship series was a battle of contrasting styles as the Mercury, number one league offense, faced the Indiana Fever, number three league defense. Phoenix won Game One in overtime, 120–116, the highest scoring game in WNBA playoffs history, and defeated the Fever in five games, capturing their second WNBA championship.[^cite_note-60]

Westhead's system influenced his Mercury team, but it also created a domino effect throughout the league. Young athletic players were capable of scoring more and playing at a faster pace. As a league, the 2010 average of 80.35 points per game was its best, surpassing the 69.2 average in the league's [inaugural season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_WNBA_season).[^cite_note-61][^cite_note-62]

### Changing of the guard (2010–2012)





[Seimone Augustus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seimone_Augustus) and [Maya Moore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maya_Moore), two cornerstones of the [Minnesota Lynx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Lynx) dynasty that won four titles between 2011 and 2017.

On October 20, 2009, the WNBA announced the [Detroit Shock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_Shock) would relocate to [Tulsa, Oklahoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulsa,_Oklahoma), to become the [Tulsa Shock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulsa_Shock).[^cite_note-63] On November 20, 2009, the WNBA announced the [Sacramento Monarchs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacramento_Monarchs) had folded due to lack of support from its current owners, the [Maloof family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maloof_family), who also owned the [Sacramento Kings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacramento_Kings) at the time.[^cite_note-64] The league announced it would seek new owners to relocate the team to the [San Francisco Bay Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Francisco_Bay_Area); however, no ownership was found and a [dispersal draft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_WNBA_dispersal_draft) was held on December 14, 2009.[^cite_note-65][^cite_note-66] The folding of the Monarchs left the WNBA with 12 teams, a number that remained unchanged for the next fifteen seasons.[^cite_note-67]

The 2010 season saw a tight race in the [East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Conference_\(WNBA\)), which held a .681 winning percentage over the [West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Conference_\(WNBA\)), its highest ever. However, it was a Western team, the [Seattle Storm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Storm), who ultimately dominated the league. Led once again by the duo of [Sue Bird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sue_Bird) and [Lauren Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lauren_Jackson), the only players remaining from the 2004 championship team, the Storm finished 28–6 in the regular season and 7–0 in the playoffs.[^cite_note-68] Although the Storm swept the [Atlanta Dream](13930487.md), the [2010 Finals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_WNBA_Finals) were a close affair, with Seattle winning the three games by a combined eight points.[^cite_note-69] The Dream, who had finished their inaugural 2008 season 4–30, made a quick turnaround behind the 2009 first-overall pick [Angel McCoughtry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angel_McCoughtry) and advanced to the Finals in their third year.[^cite_note-70][^cite_note-71]

After the 2010 season, President Donna Orender announced she would be resigning from her position as of December 31.[^cite_note-\:26-72] On April 21, 2011, NBA commissioner [David Stern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Stern) announced that former [Girl Scouts of the USA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Girl_Scouts_of_the_USA) Senior Vice President and Chief Marketing Officer, [Laurel J. Richie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laurel_J._Richie), would assume duties as president on May 16, 2011.[^cite_note-73]

During their first 11 seasons in the league, the [Minnesota Lynx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Lynx) saw little success, winning only one playoff game. Before the 2010 season, [Cheryl Reeve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheryl_Reeve) took over as head coach, and the team added [Rebekkah Brunson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rebekkah_Brunson) and [Lindsay Whalen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lindsay_Whalen) to support 2006 first-overall pick [Seimone Augustus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seimone_Augustus). The Lynx failed to make the playoffs again in Reeve's first season, but were able to select [Maya Moore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maya_Moore) with the first pick in the 2011 draft. The core of Augustus, Brunson, Moore, and Whalen would go on to make six Finals appearances and win four championships over the next seven years. Their first title came immediately in 2011, with the Lynx dominating the regular season and dropping only one game throughout their playoff run.[^cite_note-74] In the [2011 Finals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_WNBA_Finals), the Dream were swept for the second straight year despite efforts by McCoughtry, who set still-standing league records for most points in a playoff game (42) and in a Finals game (38) during the Dream's consecutive Finals appearances.[^cite_note-75]

The Lynx made it back to the [2012 Finals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_WNBA_Finals) but fell in four games to the [Indiana Fever](240214.md), led by [Tamika Catchings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamika_Catchings). In a challenging run, the Fever had to stave off elimination in both of the first two rounds and lost their second-leading scorer, [Katie Douglas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katie_Douglas_\(basketball\)), to injury before ultimately capturing their first WNBA championship.[^cite_note-76][^cite_note-77]

### The "Three to See" (2013–2019)



[Brittney Griner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brittney_Griner)



[Elena Delle Donne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elena_Delle_Donne)



[Skylar Diggins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skylar_Diggins)

Top three picks of the [2013 WNBA draft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_WNBA_draft), labeled "The Three To See".

The much-publicized [2013 WNBA draft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_WNBA_draft) produced [Baylor Bears](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baylor_Bears_women's_basketball) center [Brittney Griner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brittney_Griner), [Delaware Fightin' Blue Hens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware_Fightin'_Blue_Hens_women's_basketball) forward [Elena Delle Donne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elena_Delle_Donne), and [Notre Dame Fighting Irish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Notre_Dame_Fighting_Irish_women's_basketball) guard [Skylar Diggins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skylar_Diggins) as the top three picks. It was the first WNBA draft televised in prime time on ESPN after the league extended its TV deal with the network through 2022.[^cite_note-78] Griner, Delle Donne, and Diggins were thus labeled "The Three To See".[^cite_note-79][^cite_note-80]

The retirement of legends [Ticha Penicheiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ticha_Penicheiro), [Katie Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katie_Smith), [Sheryl Swoopes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheryl_Swoopes), and [Tina Thompson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tina_Thompson)[^cite_note-81][^cite_note-82] coupled with the arrival of highly touted rookies and new rule changes effectively marked the end of an era for the WNBA and the ushering of another.[^cite_note-83] The promotion of "The Three To See" helped boost TV ratings for the league by 28%, and half of the teams ended the 2013 season profitable.[^cite_note-84][^cite_note-85] The improved health of the league was on display after the season; when the [Los Angeles Sparks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_Sparks)' ownership group folded, it took the league only a few weeks to line up [Guggenheim Partners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guggenheim_Partners) to purchase the team, and the franchise also garnered interest from the ownership of the [Golden State Warriors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_State_Warriors).[^cite_note-86] On the court, the [Minnesota Lynx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Lynx) won their second title in three years, sweeping the [Atlanta Dream](13930487.md) in the [2013 Finals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_WNBA_Finals).[^cite_note-87]

This narrative was reinforced by the [2014 Finals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_WNBA_Finals), which featured a matchup between the [Phoenix Mercury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenix_Mercury) (led by Griner and [Diana Taurasi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diana_Taurasi)) and the [Chicago Sky](2717051.md) (led by Delle Donne and [Sylvia Fowles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sylvia_Fowles)) and became the most-watched series since 2003.[^cite_note-88] The Mercury swept the first-time finalist Sky to cap a dominant season. They went 29–5 in the regular season, the best winning percentage since the 2001 Sparks.[^cite_note-89]



[Sylvia Fowles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sylvia_Fowles), a two-time Finals MVP, two-time WNBA champion with the [Lynx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Lynx), and four-time Defensive Player of the Year

However, the 2015 season began without several stars from the previous Finals. Taurasi was paid by her Russian club, where she played during the WNBA offseason, to sit out the season,[^cite_note-90] while Fowles refused to play for Chicago before she was traded to the Lynx in July.[^cite_note-91] This proved enough to swing the balance, as the Lynx returned to the top by defeating the [Indiana Fever](240214.md) in five games in the [2015 Finals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_WNBA_Finals). Fowles was named Finals MVP, helping the Lynx avenge their 2012 defeat.[^cite_note-92] After the 2015 season, the [Tulsa Shock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulsa_Shock) moved to the [Dallas–Fort Worth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas%E2%80%93Fort_Worth) region and were renamed the [Dallas Wings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas_Wings).[^cite_note-93][^cite_note-94] The league also saw another change in leadership, as president [Laurel J. Richie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laurel_J._Richie) left her position in November 2015[^cite_note-\:27-95] and was succeeded by [Lisa Borders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lisa_Borders) in February 2016.[^cite_note-96]

Beginning in 2016, the league abolished conference-based playoff seeding: the top eight teams by record, regardless of conference, advanced to the postseason, with the top two regular-season teams receiving byes directly to the semifinals.[^cite_note-97] Coincidentally, the next two seasons were marked by a clear separation between two Western teams, the Lynx and the Sparks, and the rest of the league, culminating in back-to-back five-game Finals matchups.[^cite_note-98][^cite_note-99] In the [2016 Finals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_WNBA_Finals), the Sparks, led by [Candace Parker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candace_Parker) and [Nneka Ogwumike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nneka_Ogwumike), won their first championship since 2002, taking Game Five in Minnesota on a game-winning Ogwumike basket with 3.1 seconds left.[^cite_note-100] The Lynx earned their revenge in the [2017 Finals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_WNBA_Finals), defeating the Sparks in five games.[^cite_note-101] It marked Minnesota's fourth title in seven years and the final chapter of the franchise's dynasty—since after an unsuccessful 2018 season, Brunson, Moore, and Whalen all effectively retired from the league.

Following the [2017 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_WNBA_season), the [San Antonio Stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Antonio_Stars) moved to Nevada, becoming the [Las Vegas Aces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Las_Vegas_Aces).[^cite_note-102][^cite_note-103] In October 2018, Borders stepped down as president.[^cite_note-104] [Mark Tatum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Tatum) served as interim president until May 2019, when [Cathy Engelbert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathy_Engelbert) was named WNBA commissioner, a role that replaced the league's president title.[^cite_note-\:28-105]



[Breanna Stewart](32433243.md), a two-time MVP and three-time champion with the [Seattle Storm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Storm) and [New York Liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Liberty)

After their 2010 championship, the [Seattle Storm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Storm) posted one winning season in the next seven years. However, this put them in position to draft back-to-back No. 1 overall picks for the second time in franchise history: [Jewell Loyd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewell_Loyd) in 2015 and [Breanna Stewart](32433243.md) in 2016. Alongside Sue Bird, they returned the Storm to the top in 2018. Their toughest playoff test came in a five-game semifinal series against the Mercury.[^cite_note-106] In the [Finals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_WNBA_Finals), Seattle swept the first-time finalist [Washington Mystics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Mystics), led by Delle Donne, who had been traded to Washington a year earlier.[^cite_note-107]

With Stewart and Bird missing the 2019 season due to injuries, the Mystics dominated the regular season behind Delle Donne's MVP campaign and the first [50-40-90 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/50-40-90_season) in WNBA history. Led by [Mike Thibault](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Thibault), the winningest coach in WNBA history, the Mystics ran the best offense in league history,[^cite_note-108][^cite_note-109] and returned to the [Finals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_WNBA_Finals) for a second straight year. There, they faced the [Connecticut Sun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Connecticut_Sun), who reached the Finals for the first time since 2005—coincidentally, then coached by Thibault. The Mystics prevailed in five games to win their first championship in franchise history, as well as the first for Delle Donne and Thibault.[^cite_note-110]

### New collective bargaining agreement and expansion (2020–2023)

During the 2018 season, the [WNBA players' union](68077461.md) opted out of the [collective bargaining agreement](12728750.md) (CBA) with the league, which ended after the 2019 season. In January 2020, the league and union announced they had reached an agreement on a new CBA to take effect with the 2020 season and running through 2027, with an opt-out after 2025. The agreement increased player compensation by more than 50% and expanded benefits, including fully paid maternity leave. It also broadened [free‑agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_agent) rights. In addition, the CBA introduced a "prioritization" clause that penalizes veteran players who report late to WNBA training camp after returning from overseas play.[^cite_note-111][^cite_note-112][^cite_note-113]

Also in January 2020, the WNBA announced a new in-season tournament, the [Commissioner's Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_Commissioner's_Cup), originally slated to debut in the [2020 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_WNBA_season).[^cite_note-114] On April 3, the WNBA announced that due to the [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md), the beginning of the 2020 season would be postponed. The [2020 entry draft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_WNBA_draft) took place as originally scheduled on April 17, although it was done remotely.[^cite_note-115] In June, WNBA commissioner Cathy Engelbert announced plans for a 22‑game regular season beginning in July, with a traditional playoff format, to be held exclusively at [IMG Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IMG_Academy) in [Bradenton, Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bradenton,_Florida). The players were housed at the Bradenton complex, and all games and practices took place there.[^cite_note-116] Several players opted out for medical or personal reasons.[^cite_note-117] The reigning champion Mystics were particularly affected, with Delle Donne among those absent, and barely made the playoffs. The shortened regular season was largely dominated by the [Seattle Storm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Storm) and the [Las Vegas Aces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Las_Vegas_Aces), who ultimately met in the [Finals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_WNBA_Finals). It was the Aces franchise's first Finals appearance since 2008, and their first since relocating to Las Vegas. The Storm, back at full strength, swept their playoff run to win their second title in three years.[^cite_note-118]

On March 15, 2021, it was announced that the WNBA would introduce a ceremonial logo, basketball, and uniforms as part of its 25th anniversary celebratory campaign called "Count It".[^cite_note-119] Also for the campaign, the league unveiled [The W25](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_W25), a list of 25 players determined to be the league's greatest and most influential, as chosen by a panel of media and pioneering women's players.[^cite_note-120]



[Candace Parker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candace_Parker), the only player to win the MVP award as a rookie and the only player to win championships with three different teams.

The [2021 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_WNBA_season) largely returned to normal conditions and featured a 32‑game regular season with a month‑long Olympic break for the postponed [2020 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Summer_Olympics).[^cite_note-\:16-121] The Commissioner's Cup officially debuted that year.[^cite_note-122] The [inaugural Cup Final](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_WNBA_Commissioner's_Cup), held on August 12 as the first game after the Olympic break, was streamed via [Amazon Prime Video](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amazon_Prime_Video), and the reigning WNBA champions, the [Seattle Storm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Storm), won the first edition by defeating the [Connecticut Sun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Connecticut_Sun).[^cite_note-123] Neither team replicated its success in the playoffs, and in a season full of twists, the [2021 WNBA Finals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_WNBA_Finals) produced a rematch of the 2014 Finals between the fifth-seeded [Phoenix Mercury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenix_Mercury) and the sixth-seeded [Chicago Sky](2717051.md). Benefiting from expanded free‑agency rights under the new CBA, the Sky were able to sign [Candace Parker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candace_Parker) before the season to bolster their established core.[^cite_note-124] Chicago prevailed in four games to win their first championship in franchise history.[^cite_note-125]

In February 2022, the league raised \$75 million in capital, on terms valuing the league at \$475M. Under the deal, investors received 16% of the league's equity. In mid-2022, commissioner Cathy Engelbert confirmed that the league was actively considering expansion.[^cite_note-\:2-126]



[A'ja Wilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A'ja_Wilson), a four-time MVP and three-time champion with the [Las Vegas Aces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Las_Vegas_Aces)

Before the 2022 season, the Las Vegas Aces signed head coach [Becky Hammon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Becky_Hammon) to a then‑unprecedented \$1M annual salary.[^cite_note-127][^cite_note-128] The move was the latest in a series of ambitious investments by the team's new owner, [Mark Davis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Davis_\(American_football\)), who purchased the franchise in February 2021,[^cite_note-129] including the construction of a \$40M dedicated training facility, the first of its kind in the league.[^cite_note-130][^cite_note-131] The Aces' core included consecutive first-overall picks from 2017 to 2019—[Kelsey Plum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kelsey_Plum), [A'ja Wilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A'ja_Wilson), and [Jackie Young](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jackie_Young)—along with [Chelsea Gray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chelsea_Gray), whom they signed in free agency in 2021. In 2022, with the regular season expanded to 36 games, Las Vegas finished with the best regular‑season record and won the [Commissioner's Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_WNBA_Commissioner's_Cup). That season, the playoff format reverted to a traditional bracket without byes for top seeds and with a best‑of‑three first round. The Aces advanced to the [Finals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_WNBA_Finals) and defeated the Connecticut Sun in four games to win their first championship in franchise history.[^cite_note-132]

The 2023 season was widely framed as a battle between two "superteams": the Aces and the [New York Liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Liberty). The Aces added Candace Parker in free agency, while the Liberty signed free agents Breanna Stewart and [Courtney Vandersloot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Courtney_Vandersloot) and traded for [Jonquel Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonquel_Jones), joining 2020 first-overall pick [Sabrina Ionescu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabrina_Ionescu) in New York.[^cite_note-133] The two franchises were frequently cited as examples of increased owner investment, and both drew league scrutiny, resulting in investigations or penalties, for providing player benefits beyond those stipulated by the CBA.[^cite_note-134][^cite_note-135][^cite_note-136] That year, the league expanded the regular‑season schedule to 40 games. The season largely matched expectations: the two teams met in the [Commissioner's Cup Final](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_WNBA_Commissioner's_Cup), which the Liberty won, and then again in the [WNBA Finals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_WNBA_Finals).[^cite_note-137] Despite playing without Parker throughout the playoffs and losing two starters to injury during the Finals, the Aces defeated the Liberty in four games and became the first repeat champions since the 2001–02 [Los Angeles Sparks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_Sparks).[^cite_note-138] The regular season was the most-watched in 21 years, and the Finals were the most-watched since 2003.[^cite_note-139][^cite_note-140]

On October 5, 2023, the WNBA announced an agreement with the owners of the NBA's [Golden State Warriors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_State_Warriors) to bring a team to [San Francisco](49728.md), with a reported \$50M expansion fee payable over 10 years.[^cite_note-141][^cite_note-142] The [Golden State Valkyries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_State_Valkyries) became the league's first expansion team since the [Atlanta Dream](13930487.md) in 2008 and joined as the 13th team in 2025.[^cite_note-143] On May 23, 2024, the league announced that the Kilmer Group had acquired a franchise based in [Toronto](64646.md) that would debut in 2026.[^cite_note-144] On September 18, 2024, the league announced that the Bhathal family, owners of the [Portland Thorns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portland_Thorns) of the [National Women's Soccer League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Women's_Soccer_League) (NWSL), had acquired a franchise based in [Portland](23503.md) that would also debut in 2026.[^cite_note-145][^cite_note-146]

### Popularity surge and further expansion plans (2024–present)



[Caitlin Clark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caitlin_Clark)



[Angel Reese](70605969.md)



[Paige Bueckers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paige_Bueckers)

Clark, Reese, and Bueckers were widely viewed as three of the leading new faces of the 2024–25 popularity surge.

The [2024 WNBA draft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_WNBA_draft) drew unprecedented attention amid surging interest and record viewership in women's [college basketball](768856.md), particularly the [2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_NCAA_Division_I_women's_basketball_championship_game) and [2024 NCAA championship games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_NCAA_Division_I_women's_basketball_championship_game). The draft class was led by first-overall pick [Caitlin Clark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caitlin_Clark), who is widely regarded as [the main driver of the surge in attention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caitlin_Clark_effect), and featured other highly followed players, including [Angel Reese](70605969.md). The draft averaged 2.45M viewers, more than four times the previous record for a WNBA draft.[^cite_note-147] It was the first of numerous viewership records set during the record-setting [2024 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_WNBA_season), the majority of which involved Clark's [Indiana Fever](240214.md); games featuring both [Clark and Reese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clark%E2%80%93Reese_rivalry) proved a particular draw.[^cite_note-148][^cite_note-149] The [2024 All-Star Game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_WNBA_All-Star_Game) averaged 3.44M viewers, delivering the league's largest audience since its opening weekend in 1997.[^cite_note-150] Attendance also rose, with the league and individual teams setting multiple highs, including a regular‑season record of 20,711 for a Mystics–Fever game in Washington.[^cite_note-151][^cite_note-152] In July 2024, the league announced a new 11-year media deal worth \$2.2 billion.[^cite_note-153]

In 2024, the reigning champions, the [Las Vegas Aces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Las_Vegas_Aces), faltered despite a historic season by [A'ja Wilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A'ja_Wilson).[^cite_note-154] The [New York Liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Liberty) and the [Minnesota Lynx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Lynx) squared off in both the [Commissioner's Cup Final](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_WNBA_Commissioner's_Cup), which the Lynx won, and the [WNBA Finals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_WNBA_Finals). The five-game Finals were one of the closest in history, with two games going to overtime and two others decided by a single possession. For the first time in WNBA Finals history, a decisive Game Five went to overtime. The Liberty ultimately prevailed, after a controversial ending,[^cite_note-155][^cite_note-156][^cite_note-157] to win their first championship in franchise history.[^cite_note-158] The playoffs were no exception to the rising interest, and the Finals drew their largest audience in 25 years.[^cite_note-159]

With the [Golden State Valkyries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_State_Valkyries) joining the league, the [2025 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_WNBA_season) expanded to 44 games. Despite Clark and other stars missing significant time due to injuries, the league largely sustained upward trends in interest, viewership, and attendance,[^cite_note-160][^cite_note-161][^cite_note-162] highlighted by the Valkyries immediately setting attendance records.[^cite_note-163] Investment continued to rise across the league, and by the end of 2025, more than half of franchises had either opened or announced dedicated training facilities.[^cite_note-164] On August 15, 2025, the [Seattle Storm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Storm) and the [Atlanta Dream](13930487.md) played the first-ever international WNBA regular-season game at [Rogers Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rogers_Arena) in [Vancouver](32706.md).[^cite_note-165] The Lynx topped the regular‑season standings, but were upset by the Fever in the [Commissioner's Cup Final](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_WNBA_Commissioner's_Cup) and by the [Phoenix Mercury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenix_Mercury) in the playoffs. In the [WNBA Finals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_WNBA_Finals), played in a best-of-seven format for the first time, the Mercury faced the Aces. Las Vegas struggled midseason but closed the regular season on a 16-game win streak,[^cite_note-166] with Wilson earning a record fourth MVP.[^cite_note-167] The Aces swept the Mercury to claim their third title in four years.[^cite_note-168]

On June 30, 2025, the WNBA announced expansion teams in [Cleveland](5951.md), [Detroit](8687.md), and [Philadelphia](50585.md). The Cleveland team is scheduled to begin play in 2028, followed by Detroit in 2029, and Philadelphia in 2030.[^cite_note-169]

#### New Collective Bargaining Agreement

In July 2025, during the [WNBA All-Star Weekend](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_WNBA_All-Star_Game), the [WNBA players union](68077461.md) (WNBPA) and league officials had a meeting regarding a new collective bargaining agreement (CBA).[^cite_note-\:10-170] The CBA, which was signed in 2020, was set to expire in 2027, but in October 2024, the WNBPA exercised its right to opt out of the agreement, effective on October 31, 2025.[^cite_note-171] More than 40 players were present at the meeting, which they described as a "missed opportunity",[^cite_note-\:10-170] since neither side get any closer to a new deal. Meanwhile, [Engelbert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathy_Engelbert) called the conversation "very constructive".[^cite_note-172] Players had said that revenue sharing, salary structure, and prioritization were some of the key issues that they did not agree on.[^cite_note-173] On July 19, before the All-Star Game, all players wore "Pay Us What You Owe Us" T-shirts during warmups and fans chanted "pay them!" in support after the game.[^cite_note-174] In August, representatives of the WNBA and WNBPA had another meeting in New York to continue the negotiations.[^cite_note-175] On October 30, both parties agreed to an extension, pushing the deadline for the CBA expiration for 30 days.[^cite_note-176] On November 30, both agreed to a second extension, pushing the expiration deadline until January 9, 2026.[^cite_note-177] On December 18, the WNBPA announced that the union had authorized the executive committee to "call a strike when necessary" as players continued negotiations with the league over a new CBA.[^cite_note-178] If both sides hadn't reached an agreement, the league could have experienced a [lockout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockout_\(industry\)), which has never happened in its history.[^cite_note-179]

On March 24, 2026, the WNBA and its players' union finalized a new seven-year CBA extending to 2032. The agreement introduced a historic shift in the league's economic structure by directly linking player salaries to league revenue for the first time, with players receiving an average of 20% of total revenue.[^cite_note-180][^cite_note-2026_CBA_apnews-181] These changes facilitated a substantial increase in the teams' salary cap, which rose from \$1.5M under the previous deal to \$7M. In turn, player compensation saw a significant upward adjustment; the average player salary reached \$583,000, and the supermax contract was established at \$1.4M. Minimum salaries were also restructured to range between \$270,000 and \$300,000 based on a player's years of service.[^cite_note-2026_CBA_apnews-181]

The 2026 CBA also introduced key changes to the "Core" player designation, which allows a team to retain exclusive negotiating rights with a star player who would otherwise become an unrestricted free agent, in exchange for a one-year qualifying offer at the league's supermax salary level. The total number of times a player can be "cored" during their career was reduced from three to two. Furthermore, a service-year limit was established to ensure that, starting in 2027, players with seven or more years of experience can't be designated as core players, allowing them to reach unrestricted free agency.[^cite_note-2026_CBA_si-182]

The 2026 agreement introduced the "Exceptional Performance on Initial Contract" (EPIC) provision. This allows players to renegotiate the fourth year of their rookie scale contract and agree to a three-year extension if they meet specific performance criteria. Players who earn All-WNBA First or Second Team honors become eligible for a maximum salary in that fourth year, while those who win the league's Most Valuable Player award qualify for the supermax salary.[^cite_note-2026_CBA_si-182]

[   ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:Map/4/38/-94.7/en)

The WNBA originated with eight teams in 1997.[^cite_note-\:15-183] Through a sequence of expansions, contractions, and relocations, it will consist of 15 teams as of the [2026 season](81297710.md).[^cite_note-184]

As of the 2026 season, the [Las Vegas Aces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Las_Vegas_Aces) (formerly known as the [Utah Starzz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utah_Starzz) and [San Antonio (Silver) Stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Antonio_Stars)), [Los Angeles Sparks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_Sparks), [New York Liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Liberty), and [Phoenix Mercury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenix_Mercury) are the only remaining franchises that were founded in 1997.[^cite_note-\:15-183]

Arenas listed below reflect those in use during the 2026 season.

### Relationship with NBA teams

#### Share ownership

WNBA teams affiliated with an [NBA](22093.md) team from the same market are known as sister teams. These include:[^cite_note-187]

- The Golden State [Valkyries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_State_Valkyries) and [Warriors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_State_Warriors)

- The Indiana [Fever](240214.md) and [Pacers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_Pacers)

- The Los Angeles [Sparks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_Sparks) and [Lakers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_Lakers)

- The Minnesota [Lynx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Lynx) and [Timberwolves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Timberwolves)

- The [New York Liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Liberty) and [Brooklyn Nets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brooklyn_Nets)

- The Phoenix [Mercury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenix_Mercury) and [Suns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenix_Suns)

- The Washington [Mystics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Mystics) and [Wizards](72858.md)

- Starting in the 2027 season, the second Houston Comets, formerly the [Connecticut Sun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Connecticut_Sun), and the [Houston Rockets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Rockets)

- The Cleveland [Sirens](80322834.md) (2028) and [Cavaliers](72868.md)

- Future expansion team [Detroit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_WNBA_team) (2029) and the [Pistons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_Pistons)

- Future expansion team [Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_WNBA_team) (2030) and the [76ers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_76ers)

Of these teams, only the Mystics don't share an arena with their NBA counterpart; the Mystics primarily play at [CareFirst Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CareFirst_Arena), although they also play select games at [Capital One Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_One_Arena) when facing high-profile opponents.[^cite_note-188]

The Liberty were previously associated with the [New York Knicks](72855.md), having been owned by the Knicks' parent company, the [Madison Square Garden Sports Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madison_Square_Garden_Company),[^cite_note-189][^cite_note-190] but the team was sold in January 2019 to a group led by [Joseph Tsai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Tsai), then a minority owner of the Nets[^cite_note-191] and now sole owner of that team.[^cite_note-192][^cite_note-193] The Liberty, which played at [Madison Square Garden](75129.md) until 2017 and at [Westchester County Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westchester_County_Center) in suburban White Plains in 2018 and 2019,[^cite_note-194][^cite_note-195] were then relocated to the Nets' home arena, [Barclays Center](1658814.md), in downtown Brooklyn in 2020.[^cite_note-196][^cite_note-197]

From 1997 to 2007, the [Buss family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerry_Buss) owned and operated both the Sparks and Lakers, but in 2007, they sold the Sparks to an investment group led by Kathy Goodman and Carla Cristofferson.[^cite_note-\:3-198] In 2014, the Sparks were then sold to a group led by Lakers legend [Magic Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magic_Johnson),[^cite_note-\:3-198] and after Johnson's investment partner [Mark Walter](35264441.md) bought majority shares of the Lakers from the Buss family in 2025, both teams became sister teams again.[^cite_note-199]

&#x20; \


#### Share market with an NBA team, but not ownership

There are several WNBA teams that are in the same market as an NBA team, but are independently owned:&#x20;

- The [Atlanta Dream](13930487.md) shared [State Farm Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Farm_Arena) with the [Hawks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlanta_Hawks) from 2008 to 2016 and again in 2019,

  [^cite_note-200]

  &#x20;and currently shares the [Gateway Center Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gateway_Center_Arena) with the Hawks' [G League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NBA_G_League) affiliate, the [College Park Skyhawks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_Park_Skyhawks);

  [^cite_note-201]

  &#x20;however, the Hawks have never held any ownership stake in the Dream.

- The [Chicago Sky](2717051.md) are not affiliated with the [Bulls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Bulls) and play in a separate arena from their NBA counterpart.

  [^cite_note-202]

  [^cite_note-203]

- The [Detroit Shock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_Shock) was the sister team of the [Pistons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_Pistons)

  [^cite_note-204]

  &#x20;until the teams' owner sold the Shock to investors who [moved the team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulsa_Shock) to [Tulsa, Oklahoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulsa,_Oklahoma). The franchise relocated again in 2016, this time to the [Dallas–Fort Worth metroplex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas%E2%80%93Fort_Worth_metroplex) to become the [Dallas Wings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas_Wings).

  [^cite_note-205]

  &#x20;However, the Wings are not affiliated with the [Mavericks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas_Mavericks) and also play in a separate arena.

  [^cite_note-206]

  [^cite_note-207]

- The [Portland Fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portland_Fire) plays in the [Trail Blazers](72897.md)' home of [Moda Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moda_Center), but unlike the [previous team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portland_Fire_\(2000%E2%80%932002\)),

  [^cite_note-208]

  &#x20;does not share ownership with the NBA team. The WNBA team instead shares ownership with [Portland Thorns FC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portland_Thorns_FC) of the [National Women's Soccer League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Women's_Soccer_League).

  [^cite_note-209]

- The [Toronto Tempo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toronto_Tempo) are principally owned by [Larry Tanenbaum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Larry_Tanenbaum) independent of the [Raptors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toronto_Raptors)' parent company [Maple Leaf Sports & Entertainment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maple_Leaf_Sports_&_Entertainment) (MLSE), although Tanenbaum was the former chairman for MLSE.

  [^cite_note-Ahmed-210]

  &#x20;[Rogers Communications](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rogers_Communications), the majority owner of MLSE, intends to buy out Tanenbaum's share of the company by summer 2026.

  [^cite_note-211]

#### Do not share market with an NBA team

Three teams do not share a market with an NBA counterpart:&#x20;

- The [Orlando Miracle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orlando_Miracle) was the sister team of the [Magic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orlando_Magic) until the team was sold to the [Mohegan Sun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohegan_Sun) casino, who relocated the franchise to [Connecticut](6466.md) to become the [Sun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Connecticut_Sun).

  [^cite_note-212]

  &#x20;However, they will be relocating to Houston to return as the [Houston Comets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Comets) in 2027 and will have the same owners as the [Houston Rockets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Rockets).

- The [Utah Starzz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utah_Starzz) were affiliated with the [Jazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utah_Jazz) before relocating to San Antonio as the [Silver Stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Antonio_Stars) in 2003. The Silver Stars (shortened to Stars in 2014) were then paired with the [San Antonio Spurs](72889.md) from 2003 through the 2017 season, but that relationship ended in October 2017 when the Stars were bought by [MGM Resorts International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MGM_Resorts_International) and moved to [Las Vegas](47737.md) to become the [Aces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Las_Vegas_Aces).

  [^cite_note-Stars_move2-213]

- The [Seattle Storm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Storm) was formerly the sister team of the [SuperSonics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_SuperSonics), but was sold to a Seattle-based group when the SuperSonics [relocated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_SuperSonics_relocation_to_Oklahoma_City) to become the [Oklahoma City Thunder](18256220.md).

  [^cite_note-214]

  [^cite_note-215]

## Season format

### Regular season

Teams hold training camps in April and May. These allow the coaching staff to prepare the players for the regular season and determine the 12-woman roster with which they will begin the regular season. A series of preseason games is also played during this period.[^cite_note-216][^cite_note-217]

The WNBA regular season begins in May. In 2023 and 2024, each team played 40 regular-season games, 20 each home and away. With the [Golden State Valkyries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_State_Valkyries) joining the league in 2025, the regular season expanded to 44 games.[^cite_note-2025format-218] As with the [NBA](22093.md), each team hosts and visits every other team at least once every season. In 2027, the regular season will expand to 50 games.[^cite_note-219]

During years in which the [Summer Olympics](27566.md) are held, the WNBA takes a month off in the middle of the season to allow players to practice and compete with their respective national teams.[^cite_note-\:16-121] During years in which the [FIBA World Cup](5779563.md) was held, the WNBA would either take a break for the World Cup or end its season early, depending on the World Cup's scheduling. However, as of mid-2025, it was announced that the World Cup would move back its calendar starting in 2030 to avoid overlap with the WNBA season.[^cite_note-\:18-220][^cite_note-221]

### Commissioner's Cup

Main article: [WNBA Commissioner's Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_Commissioner's_Cup)

The 2020 season was planned to be the first for the [Commissioner's Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_Commissioner's_Cup), an in-season tournament. Each team's first home and away games against each of its conference opponents, all of which were to be played in the first half of the season, were designated as Cup games. After each team played its 10 Cup games, the top team in each conference's Cup standings would advance to the Commissioner's Cup Final, a single match held in August. The [COVID-19 pandemic](63136490.md) caused the tournament to be delayed, instead launching in 2021 under the originally announced format.[^cite_note-222]

The format was changed for the 2024 season. Each team now plays only one Commissioner's Cup game against each team in its conference, with either two or three games at home and the remainder away. All games in the first half of June are Cup contests. The Cup final, which remains a single game featuring the top teams in each conference's Cup standings, is now held near the end of June or at the start of July. Under both formats, all Cup games except the final count as regular-season games.[^cite_note-223]

### All-Star Game

Main article: [WNBA All-Star Game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_All-Star_Game)

In July, the regular season pauses to celebrate the annual All-Star Game. The first edition was held in [1999](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1999_WNBA_All-Star_Game), the league's third season. The game is part of a weekend-long event, held in a selected WNBA city each year and played on the selected WNBA team's home court. Through the 2017 edition, the All-Star Game featured star players from the [Western Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Conference_\(WNBA\)) facing star players from the [Eastern Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Conference_\(WNBA\)). Since 2018, conference affiliations have been ignored in team selections. During the season, voting for All-Star starters takes place among fans, WNBA players, and sports media members. The starters are selected by a weighted vote (fans 50%, players and media 25% each), while reserves are selected by the league's head coaches. The two players with the most fan votes are named team captains, who then fill out their teams in a draft format.[^cite_note-224]

In 2004, [The Game at Radio City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Game_at_Radio_City) was held in place of a traditional All-Star Game.[^cite_note-225][^cite_note-226] The 2006 All-Star Game was the first game to feature custom uniforms that matched the decade anniversary logo.[^cite_note-227] From 2008 through 2016, no All-Star Game was held in any Summer Olympic year.[^cite_note-\:17-228][^cite_note-229] In 2010, an exhibition game (called [Stars at the Sun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stars_at_the_Sun)) was held.[^cite_note-230] Although the [2020 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Summer_Olympics) were postponed to 2021 due to [COVID-19](62750956.md), no All-Star Game was played in that season. The 2021 season featured the first [All-Star Game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_WNBA_All-Star_Game) in an Olympic year since 2000; this contest featured a WNBA all-star team facing the [US national team](7129278.md).[^cite_note-\:17-228] The [2024 game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_WNBA_All-Star_Game) used the same format as in 2021, and was also an official All-Star Game.[^cite_note-231]

Shortly after the All-Star break is the [trading deadline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trading_deadline). After this date, teams are not allowed to exchange players with each other for the remainder of the season—although, they may still sign and release players. Major trades are often completed right before the trading deadline.[^cite_note-232]

### Playoffs

Main article: [WNBA playoffs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_playoffs)

The WNBA playoffs usually begin in September,[^cite_note-\:19-233] though in years of the [FIBA World Cup](5779563.md) they may begin in August. (However, the World Cup announced it would move its calendar starting in 2030 to avoid overlap with the WNBA.[^cite_note-\:18-220]) In the current system, the eight best teams by the regular-season record, without regard to conference alignment, qualify for the playoffs. Since 2022, the playoffs have been held in a standard knockout format, with the quarterfinals consisting of best-of-three series and the semifinals being best-of-five. The Finals were also best-of-five until being expanded to best-of-seven in 2025.[^cite_note-2022format-234] Since 2021, [Google](1092923.md) has been the official sponsor of all playoff rounds except for the Finals,[^cite_note-235][^cite_note-236] which are sponsored by [YouTube TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/YouTube_TV).[^cite_note-2025format-218]

Having a higher seed offers several advantages, including typically facing a weaker team and having home-court advantage in each round. In 2024, all quarterfinal series used a 2–1 home-court pattern, which allowed the higher seed the opportunity to win the series without having to visit the lower seed. This meant a lower seed that won one of the first two games would host the series decider.[^cite_note-237] However, the quarterfinals returned to an odd-even pattern in 2025, with the higher seed hosting the first game and a potential third game.[^cite_note-238][^cite_note-\:20-239]

The quarterfinals are bracketed in the normal manner for an 8-team tournament, with 1 vs. 8 and 4 vs. 5 on one side of the bracket and 2 vs. 7 and 3 vs. 6 on the other.[^cite_note-\:20-239] The winners of each quarterfinal series advance to the semifinals, with the bracket not being reseeded. The semifinals use a 2–2–1 home-court pattern, meaning that the higher-seeded team will have home court in games 1, 2, and 5 while the other team plays at home in game 3 and 4.[^cite_note-\:19-233]

### Finals

Main article: [WNBA Finals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_Finals)

The final playoff round, a best-of-seven series between the two semifinal winners, is known as the WNBA Finals and is held annually, currently scheduled in October.[^cite_note-\:19-233] Each player on the winning team receives a championship ring.[^cite_note-240] Also, the league awards a [WNBA Finals Most Valuable Player (MVP)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_Finals_Most_Valuable_Player_Award).[^cite_note-\:21-241] Since 2025, the Finals have used the same 2–2–1–1–1 pattern currently used by the NBA, meaning that the higher seed hosts games 1 and 2, the lower seed hosts games 3 and 4, and the teams alternate the last three games, all if necessary.[^cite_note-\:19-233]

Main articles: [List of Women's National Basketball Association players](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Women's_National_Basketball_Association_players) and [List of Women's National Basketball Association head coaches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Women's_National_Basketball_Association_head_coaches)

See also: [List of foreign WNBA players](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_foreign_WNBA_players)



[Cheryl Reeve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheryl_Reeve) Head Coach and President of Basketball Operations of the [Minnesota Lynx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Lynx), known for her coaching career and leadership in the WNBA.

In 2011, a decade and a half after the launch of the WNBA, only two players remained from the league's [inaugural season in 1997](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_WNBA_season): [Sheryl Swoopes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheryl_Swoopes)[^cite_note-242][^cite_note-243] and [Tina Thompson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tina_Thompson).[^cite_note-244][^cite_note-245] [Lisa Leslie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lisa_Leslie) was the longest-tenured player from the 1997 draft class; she spent her entire career (1997–2009) with the [Los Angeles Sparks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_Sparks).[^cite_note-246] [Sue Bird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sue_Bird) holds both of the league's most significant longevity records—number of seasons in the league (19) and games played (580).[^cite_note-247][^cite_note-248]

The members of the [WNBA's All-Decade Team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA's_All-Decade_Team) were chosen in 2006, on the WNBA's 10th anniversary, from 30 nominees compiled by fans, media, coach, and player voting. The team was to comprise the 10 best and most influential players of the first decade of the WNBA, with consideration also given to sportsmanship, community service, leadership, and contribution to the growth of women's basketball.[^cite_note-249][^cite_note-250] Players for the [WNBA's Top 15 Team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA's_Top_15_Team) were chosen in 2011 on the anniversary of the league's 15th season from amongst 30 nominees compiled similarly to that of the All-Decade Team process.[^cite_note-251] This process was repeated for the league's 20th anniversary season in 2016 with the selection of the [WNBA Top 20@20](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_Top_20@20),[^cite_note-252] and for the 25th anniversary season in 2021 with the selection of [The W25](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_W25).[^cite_note-253]



[Michele Timms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michele_Timms), known for her playmaking and leadership on the court.

Over [30 players](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_Career_Scoring) have scored at least 3,000 points in their WNBA careers. Only 15 WNBA players have reached the 6,000 point milestone: [Diana Taurasi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diana_Taurasi), [Tina Charles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tina_Charles_\(basketball\)), [DeWanna Bonner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DeWanna_Bonner), [Tina Thompson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tina_Thompson), [Tamika Catchings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamika_Catchings), [Nneka Ogwumike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nneka_Ogwumike), [Candice Dupree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candice_Dupree), [Cappie Pondexter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cappie_Pondexter), [Sue Bird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sue_Bird), [Candance Parker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candace_Parker), [Katie Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katie_Smith), [Sylvia Fowles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sylvia_Fowles), [Lisa Leslie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lisa_Leslie), [Seimone Augustus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seimone_Augustus) and [Lauren Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lauren_Jackson).[^cite_note-254] The scoring average leader is A'ja Wilson of the [Las Vegas Aces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Las_Vegas_Aces), with an average of 21.30 points per game.[^cite_note-255]

In 2007, [Paul Westhead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Westhead) of the [Phoenix Mercury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenix_Mercury) became the first person to earn both [NBA](22093.md) and WNBA championship rings as a coach.[^cite_note-256]

In 2008, 50-year-old [Nancy Lieberman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nancy_Lieberman) became the oldest player to play in a WNBA game. She signed a seven-day contract with the [Detroit Shock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_Shock) and played one game, tallying two assists and two turnovers in nine minutes of action.[^cite_note-257][^cite_note-258] By playing in the one game Lieberman broke a record that she had set in 1997 when she was the league's oldest player at 39.[^cite_note-259] The oldest player to have participated in a full season is [Diana Taurasi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diana_Taurasi), who turned 42 early in the [2024 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_WNBA_season).[^cite_note-260]

### Awards

Around the beginning of September (or late August in Olympic and [FIBA World Cup](5779563.md) years), the regular season ends. It is during this time that voting begins for individual awards.[^cite_note-\:21-241]

- The [Sixth Player of the Year Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_Sixth_Player_of_the_Year_Award) (known before 2021 as the "Sixth Woman" award) is given to the best player coming off the bench (must have more games coming off the bench than actual games started).

  [^cite_note-WNBA6th-262]

- The [Rookie of the Year Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_Rookie_of_the_Year_Award) is given to the most outstanding first-year player.

  [^cite_note-263]

- The [Most Improved Player Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_Most_Improved_Player_Award) is given to the player who is deemed to have shown the most improvement from the previous season.

  [^cite_note-264]

- The [Defensive Player of the Year Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_Defensive_Player_of_the_Year_Award) is given to the league's best defender.

  [^cite_note-265]

- The [Kim Perrot Sportsmanship Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kim_Perrot_Sportsmanship_Award) is given to the player who shows outstanding sportsmanship on and off the court.

  [^cite_note-266]

- The [Coach of the Year Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_Coach_of_the_Year_Award) is given to the coach who has made the most positive difference to a team.

  [^cite_note-267]

- The [Most Valuable Player Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_Most_Valuable_Player_Award) is given to the player deemed the most valuable for her team that season.

  [^cite_note-268]

- The [Basketball Executive of the Year Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_Basketball_Executive_of_the_Year_Award) is presented to the team executive most instrumental in his or her team's success in that season.

  [^cite_note-269]

- The newest WNBA award, first presented to a team in 2017,

  [^cite_note-270]

  &#x20;is the season-long version of the monthly [WNBA Cares Community Assist Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_Cares_Community_Assist_Award), presented to a player for especially meritorious community service.

  [^cite_note-272]

Also named are the [All-WNBA Teams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All-WNBA_Team), the [All-Defensive Teams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_All-Defensive_Team), and the [All-Rookie Team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_All-Rookie_Team); each consists of five players. (In all cases, a tie in voting may lead to a team containing six players instead of five.)[^cite_note-273]

- There are two All-WNBA teams; each consists of five top players selected without regard to position, with first-team status being the most desirable.

  [^cite_note-274]

- There are two All-Defensive teams; since 2005, each consists of the top defenders regardless of position.

  [^cite_note-275]

  [^cite_note-276]

- There is one All-Rookie team, consisting of the top five first-year players regardless of position since 2005.

  [^cite_note-277]

  [^cite_note-278]

Notes

### Notable international players

Further information: [List of foreign WNBA players](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_foreign_WNBA_players)

A number of international players that have played in the WNBA have earned multiple all-stars or won MVP awards:

- [Brazil](3383.md) [Janeth Arcain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Janeth_Arcain), Brazil – four-time WNBA champion with the [Houston Comets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Comets) (1997–2000), one-time All-Star ([2001](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2001_WNBA_All-Star_Game)) and [Most Improved Player Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_Most_Improved_Player_Award) ([2001](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2001_WNBA_season))

  [^cite_note-326]

- [Russia](25391.md) [Elena Baranova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elena_Baranova), Russia – among the first international players in the WNBA ([1997](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_WNBA_season)), one-time All-Star ([2001](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2001_WNBA_All-Star_Game))

  [^cite_note-327]

- [China](5405.md) [Zheng Haixia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zheng_Haixia), China – first winner of the [Kim Perrot Sportsmanship Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kim_Perrot_Sportsmanship_Award), first international player to win a WNBA award ([1997](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_WNBA_season))

  [^cite_note-328]

- [Poland](22936.md) [Margo Dydek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margo_Dydek), Poland – first international player to be No. 1 draft pick ([1998](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_WNBA_draft)), tallest player in WNBA history (7ft 2in tall)

  [^cite_note-329]

  [^cite_note-330]
