Some of these players, among them Jones, Lyttle, Penicheiro, Sabally, Sutton-Brown, and Young played U.S. college basketball.

## Rules and regulations

Rules are governed by standard [basketball rules](3921.md#Rules_and_regulations) as defined by the NBA, with a few notable exceptions:

- WNBA and NBA team sizes differ, with 12 players per WNBA roster and 15 per NBA roster.

  [^cite_note-\:23-342]

- The three-point line is 22 ft 1.75 in (6.75 m) from the center of the basket, with a distance of 22 ft (6.71 m) at the corners. The main arc is essentially identical to that used by [FIBA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIBA) (effective October 1, 2012, for domestic competitions) and the [NCAA](60706.md) (effective in 2019–20 in Division I and 2020–21 in Divisions II and III for men, and in 2021–22 for all women's divisions). The WNBA corner distance, as measured from the center of the basket, is identical to that of the [NBA](22093.md); the FIBA and NCAA distance at the corners is 4 in (10.16 cm) shorter.

  [^cite_note-\:23-342]

- The regulation WNBA ball is a minimum 28.5 inches (72 cm) in circumference and weighs 20.0 ounces (570 g), 1 inch (2.5 cm) smaller and 2 ounces (57 g) lighter than the NBA ball.

  [^cite_note-\:23-342]

  &#x20;Since 2004, this size has been used for all senior-level women's competitions throughout the world in full-court basketball.

  [^cite_note-343]

  [^cite_note-344]

  &#x20;Competitions in the half-court [3x3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/3x3_basketball) variant used the women's ball until 2015, when a dedicated ball with the circumference of the women's ball but the weight of the men's ball was introduced. [Wilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilson_Sporting_Goods) became the WNBA ball supplier in 2022.

  [^cite_note-345]

  &#x20;[Spalding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spalding_\(company\)) had been the ball supplier since 1997.

  [^cite_note-346]

- WNBA quarters are 10 minutes in duration instead of the NBA's 12 minutes. As such, the WNBA gets fewer timeouts—five with one "reset timeout" for a game's final minutes—while the NBA gets seven timeouts per team per game.

  [^cite_note-\:23-342]

Games are divided into four 10-minute quarters as opposed to the league's original two 20-minute halves of play, similar to FIBA and NCAA women's college rules.[^cite_note-347]

A trend with new WNBA rules has been to match them with a similar NBA rule. Since the [2006 WNBA season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_WNBA_season):[^cite_note-WNBA_Announces_Rule_Changes_for_2006-348]

- The winner of the opening jump ball begins the 4th quarter with the ball out of bounds. The loser begins with the ball out of bounds in the second and third quarters. Previously under the two-half format, both periods started with jump balls, presumably to eliminate the possibility of a team intentionally losing the opening tip to gain the opening possession of the second half. This is not a problem under the four-quarters because the winner of the opening tip gets the opening possession of the final period.

- The [shot clock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shot_clock) was decreased from 30 to 24 seconds, matching the FIBA and NBA shot clocks. Starting in 2020, the last five seconds of the shot clock counted down in tenths of a second.

The [2007 WNBA season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_WNBA_season) brought changes that included:[^cite_note-WNBA_Announces_Rule_Changes_for_2007_Season-349]

- The amount of time that a team must move the ball across the half-court line went from 10 to 8 seconds.

- A referee can grant time-outs to either a player or the coach.

- Two free throws and possession of the ball for a clear-path-to-the-basket foul. Previously only one free throw was awarded as well as possession.

In [2012](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_WNBA_season), the WNBA added the block/charge arc under the basket. As of [2013](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_WNBA_season), the defensive three-second rule and anti-flopping guidelines were introduced.[^cite_note-350] The three-point line was also extended;[^cite_note-351] in 2017, that line extended into the corners to match the NBA's.[^cite_note-352]

Since [2017](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_WNBA_season), [Tissot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tissot) is the official timekeeper for the league, as it uses a unified game clock/shot clock system.[^cite_note-353]

### Court dimensions

All dimensions are in line with [NBA](22093.md) regulations except the main three-point arc. The three-point distance at the corners is identical in the NBA and WNBA.

| WNBA Court Dimensions |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Area | Imperial | Metric |
| Length of court (baseline to baseline) | 94 ft | 28.65 m |
| Width of court (sideline to sideline) | 50 ft | 15.24 m |
| Rim height (floor to rim) | 10 ft | 3.05 m |
| Center circle diameter | 12 ft | 3.66 m |
| Three-point line distance from center of basket | 22 ft 1.75 in | 6.75 m |
| Three-point line distance from center of basket (corners) | 22 ft | 6.71 m |
| Shaded area/Lane/Key length | 19 ft | 5.8 m |
| Shaded area/Lane/Key width | 16 ft | 4.88 m |
| Restricted area (aka "block/charge arc") (distance from center of basket) | 4 ft | 1.22 m |
| Free-throw line (distance from backboard) | 15 ft | 4.57 m |
| Free-throw half-circle radius | 6 ft | 1.83 m |
| Backboard width (side to side) | 6 ft | 1.83 m |
| Coaching box width (from baseline) | 28 ft | 8.54 m |

### Player eligibility controversy

In July 2026, [Sophie Cunningham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sophie_Cunningham) spoke out against the inclusion of trans women in women's sports, leading to rallies in support of Cunningham outside her games, and comments in favor of transgender inclusion by some coaches.[^cite_note-354][^cite_note-355][^cite_note-356] In August, former [NBA](22093.md) players [Enes Kanter Freedom](22346872.md) and [Royce White](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royce_White) declared that they would identify as trans women and apply for the 2027 WBNA draft to challenge the support of trans women in the WNBA.[^cite_note-357][^cite_note-Feinberg-2026-358]

WNBA commissioner [Cathy Engelbert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathy_Engelbert) reacted by sending a memo to teams addressing ongoing discussions around [transgender athletes](46676322.md) in women's basketball, making clear that the topic would be discussed further by a task force of team presidents and general managers at a previously scheduled meeting the following week.[^cite_note-Feinberg-2026-358] Following the meeting, a league spokesperson said in a statement: "There are no immediate eligibility matters affecting the WNBA, and we strongly denounce the bad-faith efforts to use these topics to demean or marginalize others."[^cite_note-guard-13aug2026-359]

## Business

### Finance

In 2007, teams were estimated to be losing \$1.5–\$2 million a year.[^cite_note-360] In total, the league projected losses through the [2010 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_WNBA_season) to be around \$400M.[^cite_note-361] In December 2010, [Donna Orender](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donna_Orender) said the league had its first-ever "cash flow positive" team during the 2010 season.[^cite_note-362] In 2011, three teams were profitable. In 2013, six of the league's 12 teams reported a profit.[^cite_note-363] However, in 2018, NBA Commissioner [Adam Silver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam_Silver) revealed that the WNBA had lost an average of more than \$10M annually, which would amount to at least \$280M in losses since the WNBA's inception in 1997.[^cite_note-Dixon-364]

By the [2024 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_WNBA_season), the WNBA has seen revenue grow rapidly through a variety of avenues. These included raising capital,[^cite_note-365] re-negotiating their media rights deal, expansion fees,[^cite_note-\:7-366] increased ticket sales,[^cite_note-\:6-367] and establishing new strategic partnerships and sponsorships with organizations.[^cite_note-\:8-368] Still, 40% of the revenue generated by the WNBA goes to the teams and players, with the remainder going to the NBA and outside investors. Overall, the league is expected to have lost \$50M for the 2024 season.[^cite_note-\:7-366]

In 2025, the WNBA generated enough revenue to begin [revenue sharing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revenue_sharing) with its players, marking the first time in WNBA history it was able to do so.[^cite_note-\:24-369] It is estimated that the WNBA made at least \$200M in revenue in 2025.[^cite_note-370] As part of the revenue sharing, the WNBA's 13 teams received \$8M from the league to disperse to players, while another \$8M was allocated to league marketing agreements.[^cite_note-\:24-369]

### Activism

Main article: [WNBA Social Justice Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_Social_Justice_Council)



New York Liberty WNBA Team staff wore Black Lives Matter T-Shirts during a game on June 19, 2022

In 2020, *[The New York Times](30680.md)* (*NTY*) called the WNBA "the most socially progressive pro league".[^cite_note-371]

As the league's popularity has grown, players have gained more voice and power to become activists in various fields. One of the activist players' main focuses is the inequality between men's and women's sports. Many players, such as [Brittney Griner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brittney_Griner), [Breanna Stewart](32433243.md), and [Maya Moore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maya_Moore), have spoken about equality between gender, sexual orientation, and race.[^cite_note-372] The players have also supported progressive social and political movements, such as [Black Lives Matter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Lives_Matter) (BLM) and others.[^cite_note-373]

The [Minnesota Lynx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Lynx) were early advocates. *The NYT* called [Seimone Augustus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seimone_Augustus) "one of sports' most forward-thinking and undersung activists" for her early work advocating for [marriage equality](92656.md) and [LGBTQ+](78174325.md) rights.[^cite_note-374] Before the Lynx's July 9, 2016, home game, the team held a press conference where the four co-captains wore black t-shirts with the message "Change starts with us - Justice & Accountability" on the front and the names of [Philando Castile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Killing_of_Philando_Castile) and [Alton Sterling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Killing_of_Alton_Sterling) on the back. Castile had been killed by police in a traffic stop three days before the game.[^cite_note-375]

In mid-2020, the WNBA and [WNBPA](68077461.md) formed a [Social Justice Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_Social_Justice_Council),[^cite_note-376] with civil rights advocates [Alicia Garza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alicia_Garza) and [Kimberlé Crenshaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kimberl%C3%A9_Crenshaw) acting as external advisors.[^cite_note-377] Shortly after the [George Floyd protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Floyd_protests) began, the league and union decided in 2020 to put BLM and [Say Her Name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Say_Her_Name) slogans on warmup gear and opening weekend uniforms.[^cite_note-npr_dream-378] When Atlanta Dream team owner Senator [Kelly Loeffler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kelly_Loeffler) criticized the league's support for BLM, her team wore black T-shirts with the slogan "VOTE WARNOCK", endorsing her election opponent [Raphael Warnock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raphael_Warnock), an African-American pastor who defeated Loeffler.[^cite_note-npr_dream-378]



In September 2024, players from the Las Vegas Aces wore T-shirts that read VOTE.

In 2021, during the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_on_sports), the WNBA led American professional sports teams in promoting the [COVID-19 vaccine](63319438.md).[^cite_note-\:1-379] Teams hosted vaccine clinics in their home arenas.[^cite_note-\:1-379] In April, the league and union's Social Justice Council made a [PSA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_service_announcement), *Our Health is Worth a Shot*, that aired during the WNBA draft.[^cite_note-\:1-379][^cite_note-380] In June 2021, the WNBA announced that 99% of its players had been fully vaccinated.[^cite_note-381]

In June 2024, the documentary film *[Power of the Dream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_of_the_Dream)* was released.[^cite_note-\:4-382] The film focuses on the league's work to support Black Lives Matter and the organizing to support the election of Warnock. [Sue Bird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sue_Bird) and [Nneka Ogwumike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nneka_Ogwumike) are co-producers of the film, along with [Tracee Ellis Ross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tracee_Ellis_Ross) and the director [Dawn Porter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dawn_Porter_\(filmmaker\)).[^cite_note-\:4-382]

### Sponsorships

On June 1, 2009, the [Phoenix Mercury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenix_Mercury) was the first team in WNBA history to announce a marquee sponsorship.[^cite_note-383] The team secured a partnership with [LifeLock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LifeLock) to brand their jerseys and warm-ups.[^cite_note-384] It was the first branded jersey in WNBA history. After the LifeLock deal expired, the Mercury secured a new uniform sponsorship deal with [Casino Arizona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casino_Arizona) and [Talking Stick Resort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talking_Stick_Resort) on February 3, 2014.[^cite_note-385]

Other teams eventually followed in the Mercury's footsteps; some teams feature sponsors prominently on the front of their jerseys, while others have sponsors on the upper left-hand shoulder.[^cite_note-386]

On August 22, 2011, the WNBA announced a league-wide marquee sponsorship with [Boost Mobile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boost_Mobile).[^cite_note-387] The deal would allow the Boost Mobile logo to be placed on 11 of the 12 teams' jerseys (excluding San Antonio) in addition to branding on the courts and in arenas. A source said the deal was a "multiyear, eight-figure deal".[^cite_note-388]

Before the start of the 2011 season, every team announced a new look for their uniforms. The supplier of the uniforms for the league, [Adidas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adidas), upgraded all teams to new high-tech designs, much like they did for the NBA before the start of their season.[^cite_note-389]

On April 8, 2019, the WNBA announced a multiyear marquee partnership with [AT\&T](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AT\&T), making them the first non-apparel partner to have its logo featured on the front of all 12 team jerseys. The jerseys officially debuted during the [2019 WNBA draft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_WNBA_draft).[^cite_note-390]

In 2020, the league launched WNBA Changemakers, a collective of businesses committed to the advancement of women in sports. Business partners of the collective provide direct financial investment to the WNBA as well as marketing amplification through collaborations.[^cite_note-\:8-368]

### Salaries, rosters, and collective bargaining

Before the 2009 season, the maximum team roster size was changed from 13 players (11 active and 2 inactive) to 11 players (all active). Any team that falls below nine players able to play due to injury or any other factor outside of the control of the team will, upon request, be granted a roster hardship exception allowing the team to sign an additional player or players so the team will have nine players able to play in an upcoming game or games. As soon as the injured (or otherwise sidelined) player(s) can play, the roster hardship player(s)—not any other player on the roster—must be waived. In March 2014, the WNBA and players signed a new, eight-year collective bargaining agreement, increasing the number of players on a roster to 12.[^cite_note-391]

The [WNBA draft](849824.md) is held annually every spring. The minimum age is 22 years for American players and 20 years for international players, measured as of December 31 of the calendar year of the draft. For draft purposes, "American" includes those born in the U.S., as well as those who have enrolled in a U.S. college or university, regardless of citizenship. The draft is three rounds long, with each of the 12 teams in the league (trades aside) getting three picks each. The draft order for the eight teams that made the playoffs the previous year are based on team records, and the team with the highest previous record pick last. For the remaining top four picks, a selection process similar to the [NBA draft lottery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NBA_draft_lottery) is conducted for the four teams that did not qualify for the playoffs.[^cite_note-392][^cite_note-393]

Previously, in 2008, a new six-year collective bargaining agreement was agreed upon between the players and the league. The [salary cap](364837.md) for an entire team in 2010 was \$827,000 (although it was later lowered to \$775,000). By 2013 (the sixth year under this agreement), the cap for an entire team was \$900,000. In 2010, the *minimum* salary for a player with three-plus years of experience was \$51,000 while the *maximum* salary for a six-plus year player was \$101,500 (the first time in league history that players can receive over \$100,000). The minimum salary for rookies was \$35,190.[^cite_note-394][^cite_note-395] Many WNBA players supplement their salaries by playing in [European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Women's_Basketball_League), [Australian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_National_Basketball_League), or [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_Chinese_Basketball_Association) women's basketball leagues during the WNBA offseason. The WNBA [pays its female players less](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_pay_gap) than their NBA counterparts, although this is attributed to the much greater revenues of the [NBA](22093.md); however the WNBA has been criticized for "paying its players a lower share of revenue than the NBA".[^cite_note-396]

The decision of superstar [Diana Taurasi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diana_Taurasi) to sit out the 2015 WNBA season was seen by some in the media as a harbinger of salary-related troubles in the future. The Russian club for which she was playing at the time, [UMMC Ekaterinburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UMMC_Ekaterinburg), offered her a bonus well over the league's maximum player salary to sit out that season. Taurasi accepted, largely because she had not had an offseason since playing college basketball more than a decade earlier. Such offers have often been made to star American players, including Taurasi herself, but none were accepted until Taurasi did so in 2015.[^cite_note-397]

&#x20;Another incident that led to widespread media comment on the WNBA's salary structure was the [torn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achilles_tendon_rupture) [Achilles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achilles_tendon) suffered by reigning WNBA MVP [Breanna Stewart](32433243.md) while playing for another Russian side, [Dynamo Kursk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dynamo_Kursk), in the [2019 EuroLeague Women final](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018%E2%80%9319_EuroLeague_Women). The injury came at a time when the WNBA and its players' union were preparing to negotiate a new collective bargaining agreement,[^cite_note-398] following the union's announcement in November 2018 that it would opt out of the current CBA after the 2019 season. With overseas leagues offering higher salaries than the WNBA, roughly 70% of the league's players go overseas in any given season. While these players do not necessarily play as many games as NBA players do in their seasons, even participants in the [NBA Finals](890793.md) get several months of rest in the offseason, something not available for WNBA players who also play overseas.[^cite_note-399][^cite_note-400][^cite_note-Voepel_Stewie-401] In a story on the ramifications of Stewart's injury, [Michael Voepel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Voepel) of [ESPN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ESPN) had this to say about the lead-in to the injury:[^cite_note-Voepel_Stewie-401]

> For Stewart, her 2018 went like this: playing in [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_Chinese_Basketball_Association), brief time off, [WNBA season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_WNBA_season), [World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_FIBA_Women's_Basketball_World_Cup) in the Canary Islands, brief time off, playing in Russia. She hasn't had significant recovery time since before her [senior season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015%E2%80%9316_UConn_Huskies_women's_basketball_team) at [UConn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UConn_Huskies_women's_basketball). Now, she'll have time away from playing but while going through rehab and physical therapy.

The CBA that took effect in 2020 significantly increased minimum and maximum salaries. The minimum league salary in 2020 was \$57,000 for players with less than three years of experience, and \$68,000 otherwise. For most players, the 2020 maximum salary was \$185,000; players who met specified criteria for league service had a maximum of \$215,000.[^cite_note-402] In 2026, a new seven-year deal was announced, in which the maximum salary per player became \$1.4M and the minimum salary became \$270,000–\$300,000, depending on tenure.[^cite_note-403]

WNBA players are awarded bonuses for certain achievements. Some of the bonuses given by the league (amount is per player), from 2020 to 2027 (the duration of the current CBA): WNBA champion: \$11,356; Runner-up: \$5,678; Most Valuable Player: \$15,450; All-WNBA First Team member: \$10,300; and All-Star Game participant: \$2,575.[^cite_note-404] These were only modest increases from amounts provided before 2020.[^cite_note-405]

The lack of roster space for rookies, thus hampering their professional development, became a major issue. While the 2020 CBA led to maximum player salaries nearly doubling from 2019 to 2022, the team cap only increased by slightly less than 40% in that period. Because it is a hard cap, many WNBA teams carry only 11 players on their rosters instead of the maximum 12, leading Stewart to state "We're at a tipping point ... without some easy tweaks, we are no longer a league that has 12 teams and 144 players—it's more like 133." The WNBA also has no developmental league similar to the [NBA G League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NBA_G_League); [Chiney Ogwumike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chiney_Ogwumike), a vice president of the players' union, publicly called for such a league. While roster limits have always been a significant issue in the league—between the first WNBA draft in 1997 and 2021, more than 40% of drafted players never made a roster—this became especially apparent in the 2020s. For example, the [2019](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018%E2%80%9319_NCAA_Division_I_women's_basketball_season) [Naismith Trophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naismith_College_Player_of_the_Year) winner [Megan Gustafson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megan_Gustafson) did not make an opening-day roster in 2019, and had only played in parts of the 2019–2021 seasons before being cut in the 2022 preseason. This issue gained major publicity before the start of the 2022 season. The [Minnesota Lynx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Lynx), which began that week with barely over \$12,000 of cap room, cut six players, including the 2020 Rookie of the Year [Crystal Dangerfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crystal_Dangerfield), their 2021 first-round pick [Rennia Davis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rennia_Davis), and both of their 2022 draft picks. The [Seattle Storm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Storm), whose first 2022 draft pick was in the middle of the second round, waived that pick ([Elissa Cunane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elissa_Cunane)), and the [Las Vegas Aces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Las_Vegas_Aces) waived both of their picks, one of them a first-rounder.[^cite_note-406][^cite_note-407]

Another clause in the 2020 CBA, known as the "prioritization" clause, has been viewed as a potential problem for the league. Due to overseas league commitments, a significant number of WNBA players have reported late to training camp each season. Several overseas leagues and continental club competitions overlap with WNBA training camps, and even with the start of the WNBA season. For example, 55 WNBA players missed the start of training camp in 2021, meaning most teams were unable to start practice with their full rosters.[^cite_note-Hruby-408] Starting in 2023, teams are required to fine players with more than two years of WNBA experience who miss the start of training camp. (Exemptions are provided for national team commitments, graduations, and other significant life events.) Starting in 2024, the league can penalize a veteran player who does not report to camp with a season-long suspension without pay.[^cite_note-Negley20220211-409] In a 2021 episode of a podcast hosted by [Napheesa Collier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napheesa_Collier) and [A'ja Wilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A'ja_Wilson), Collier raised the prospect of players choosing to abandon the WNBA for higher overseas salaries, telling Wilson "If I'm not making that much in the league, if it's not enough for me to survive on during the year, I'm going overseas and having the summer off."[^cite_note-Hruby-408] When Stewart re-signed with the Seattle Storm as a free agent before the 2022 season, she cited the prioritization clause as the reason she only signed a one-year contract.[^cite_note-Negley20220211-409]

#### Player revenue

Many factors play into the smaller wages WNBA players receive during their season, but two major contributors are revenue and viewership numbers and the allocation of funds by both the NBA and the WNBA. It is estimated that wages for NBA players take up 50% of the league's annual revenue, while WNBA players only receive 20% of their league's income.[^cite_note-\:13-410] Kelsey Plum said in an interview with The Residency Podcast, "We're not asking to get paid what the men get paid. We're asking to get paid the same percentage of revenue shared."[^cite_note-411][^cite_note-412]

#### Player marketing agreements

Player marketing agreements (PMAs), introduced in the 2021–22 offseason, are league contracts that pay select WNBA players for year‑round marketing and promotional work on behalf of the league.[^cite_note-\:22-413][^cite_note-414] The league was expected to spend about \$1M annually on PMAs, with funds divided among participating players and individual contracts capped at \$250,000. PMAs provide additional offseason income and are also intended to encourage players to remain stateside. Players have cited varied reasons for signing PMAs, including the need to rest or rehabilitate injuries and childcare responsibilities.[^cite_note-\:22-413] However, most top WNBA players have opted not to sign PMAs, citing concerns that the agreements limit other name, image, and likeness opportunities.[^cite_note-415]

#### NBA support

The WNBA is half-owned by the [NBA](22093.md) and receives \$10–\$15M annually to [subsidize](146719.md) the league from the NBA's yearly revenue, which in 2022, reached up to \$10 billion.[^cite_note-416] Six WNBA teams are considered sister teams to the NBA teams based in the same areas and are directly connected to those NBA teams and their markets. The NBA's allotment of funding to all WNBA teams, not just sister teams, has been discussed in many academic and sports analysis circles, with some authors arguing that the NBA should be doing more to support the WNBA.[^cite_note-\:02-417] [Sponsorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sponsor_\(commercial\)) and TV deals continue to be an area where the WNBA creates less revenue than their NBA counterparts, with the WNBA bringing in \$25M from ESPN and the NBA bringing in \$930M from [ESPN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ESPN) and [TNT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TNT_\(American_TV_network\)). Some argue that the NBA could be working harder to bridge that gap between organizations and either share more revenue from deals or help the WNBA create their own more profitable agreements.[^cite_note-\:02-417]

In July 2024, the WNBA negotiated a new media rights deal as part of a total package with the NBA valued at \$77 billion. Prior to re-negotiating their deal, the WNBA media rights were only valued at \$60M a year. Under the new deal, the WNBA can expect to earn \$200M a year for the next 11 years, more than a 300% increase over previous years,[^cite_note-sherwood.news-418] but because the media deal lumps the NBA together with the WNBA, the true valuation of the WNBA media rights remains unknown.[^cite_note-\:7-366]

### Merchandise

#### 2021

The following shows the top merchandise sales during the [2021 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_WNBA_season), based on sales through the WNBA's official online store.[^cite_note-2021_jerseys-419] However, a story by [NBC Sports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NBC_Sports) journalist Alex Azzi argued that the WNBA's merchandise rankings were misleading for multiple reasons.[^cite_note-420]

- At any given time, the WNBA has at most 144 players, but at the time of the story, "ready to ship" replica team jerseys were available for fewer than 20 of them. Many of the league's top players in the 2021 season were not among those with "ready to ship" jerseys. The only such jersey available for [Jonquel Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonquel_Jones), who would be named [league MVP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_Most_Valuable_Player_Award) shortly after the story ran, was a replica of the jersey she wore in [that season's All-Star Game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_WNBA_All-Star_Game), and not her [Connecticut Sun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Connecticut_Sun) jersey. The league's leading scorer that season, [Tina Charles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tina_Charles_\(basketball\)), had no ready-made jersey available. Also, not all of the ready-made jerseys were available in youth sizes. All other player replica jerseys must be custom-ordered, which take longer to ship, cost more, and are only available in adult sizes. The only [Minnesota Lynx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Lynx) player with a ready-made jersey, [Maya Moore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maya_Moore), had not played in the league since 2018 (and formally announced her retirement in 2023).

- Some teams, among them the Lynx, offer a larger variety of ready-made replica jerseys in their online team stores. Also, [Dick's Sporting Goods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dick's_Sporting_Goods), which entered a multi-year marketing agreement with the league shortly before the story ran, has a wider availability of such jerseys than the WNBA online store. However, the league's official merchandise rankings do not include sales through any outlets other than its online store.

- While over 80% of WNBA players are black, the top three on this list, as well as four of the top five, are white. A study by two researchers at the [University of Massachusetts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Massachusetts_Amherst) published earlier in 2021 concluded that after controlling for points and rebounds, white players individually averaged twice as many media mentions as black players during the 2020 season.

  [^cite_note-421]

  &#x20;This discrepancy was called out by white [UConn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UConn_Huskies_women's_basketball) superstar [Paige Bueckers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paige_Bueckers) during her acceptance speech at the [2021 ESPY Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_ESPY_Awards), and Azzi argued that it contributed to the perceived racial bias in the WNBA merchandise rankings.

#### 2024

The arrival of [Caitlin Clark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caitlin_Clark) in 2024 [led to unprecedented public interest in the WNBA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caitlin_Clark_effect). As such, the league's merchandise was sold in record numbers, both player-specific and league-wide, such as the orange WNBA hoodie. Clark had the most jerseys sold during the year's first half, with the rankings also featuring two other rookies: [Angel Reese](70605969.md) and [Kate Martin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kate_Martin).[^cite_note-422]

## Attendance



WNBA attendance 1997–2023

In the inaugural [1997 WNBA season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_WNBA_season), average attendance for the regular season was 9,661 attendants with 112 games played. Average attendance increased by 12% in 1998 over 150 games. By 2000, the regular WNBA season consisted of 256 games and attendance dropped with an average of 9,142 attendants per game from 2000 to 2003. Beginning in 2003, attendance dropped at a rate of about 5% year-over-year until 2006 when average attendance was 7,479. From 2007 to 2014, attendance numbers remained relatively steady with a low of 7,457 in 2012 and a high of 8,039 in 2009.[^cite_note-\:5-428]

In 2015, the WNBA's attendance per game decreased by 3.4% to 7,318. This was a record low for the WNBA since it was established in 1997. The relocation of the [San Antonio Stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Antonio_Stars) to [Freeman Coliseum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freeman_Coliseum) and the [Tulsa Shock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulsa_Shock) to Arlington likely contributed to large drops in attendance in 2015 (−37.4% and −7.2% respectively). With record low attendance, the WNBA sought to improve their branding effort for the following 20th anniversary season and the WNBA President, [Laurel J. Richie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laurel_J._Richie), discussed creating an expansion committee to evaluate if and how the WNBA should go about expanding.[^cite_note-429]

Attendance in 2016 and 2017 recovered slightly with average attendance at 7,655 and 7,716 respectively, but the 2018 and 2019 seasons each set the lowest average attendance in WNBA history (6,769 and 6,535 respectively). However, about half of the decline in attendance from 2017 to 2018 was due to the [New York Liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Liberty) moving from 19,812-seat [Madison Square Garden](75129.md) to the 5,000-seat [Westchester County Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westchester_County_Center). While the Liberty had averaged over 9,000 fans in 2017, [James Dolan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_L._Dolan), then the team's owner, noted that roughly half of the team's attendance in that season came from complimentary tickets.[^cite_note-430] Similarly in 2019, the [Washington Mystics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Mystics) moved from the 20,356-seat [Capital One Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_One_Arena) to the 4,111-seat [Entertainment and Sports Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entertainment_and_Sports_Arena). The Las Vegas Aces and New York Liberty each saw double-digit percentage losses in 2019, but half of the league's teams saw attendance increases that season, and the number of sellouts was the same in both seasons (41).[^cite_note-SBD_2019-431]

With plans to expand the number of regular season games played by each team from 34 to 36, and with the introduction of the mid-season [Commissioner's Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_Commissioner's_Cup) tournament to be broadcast on [ESPN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ESPN) and [ABC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ABC_News_\(United_States\)), the league had high hopes for the [2020 WNBA season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_WNBA_season). This was derailed when the season was postponed due to the [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md) on April 3. Instead, the league held an abbreviated 22-game regular season at [IMG Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IMG_Academy) in [Bradenton, Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bradenton,_Florida), with no fans in attendance. The [2021 WNBA season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_WNBA_season) was also shortened to a 32-game regular season and attendance numbers remained low with an average of 2,620 people in attendance per game, likely due to persistent fears around the [COVID-19](63030231.md) virus and new safety protocols for large indoor venues. By 2022, average attendance rebounded to 5,646 and in 2023 attendance recovered to pre-pandemic numbers with 6,615 average attendance per game.[^cite_note-\:5-428]

2024 was a breakout season for WNBA attendance, with an average of 9,807 fans per game—an increase of 48% over the 2023 season. Three games drew more than 20,000 fans, including a record-breaking attendance of 20,711 fans when the [Indiana Fever](240214.md) visited the [Washington Mystics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Mystics) on September 19.[^cite_note-\:6-367] A surge in interest in women's college basketball preceded the [2024 WNBA season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_WNBA_season), with viewership of the 2024 women's [NCAA](536018.md) [championship game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_NCAA_Division_I_women's_basketball_championship_game) up over 90% from [the previous season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_NCAA_Division_I_women's_basketball_championship_game), marking the first time in [NCAA](60706.md) history where viewership for the women's championship game exceeded the [men's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_championship_game).[^cite_note-432] Sports analysts cited intense interest in the incoming 2024 rookie class, led by number one draft pick [Caitlin Clark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caitlin_Clark), as the reason for the dramatic increases in attendance, dubbing her ability to drive ticket sales "[Clarkonomics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caitlin_Clark_effect)".[^cite_note-433]

## Media coverage

Main articles: [WNBA on ESPN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_on_ESPN), [WNBA on ABC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_on_ABC), [WNBA on Lifetime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_on_Lifetime), [WNBA on Oxygen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_on_Oxygen), [WNBA on NBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_on_NBC), [NBA on Prime Video](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NBA_on_Prime_Video), [WNBA on CBS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_on_CBS), and [WNBA on USA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_on_USA)



[Rebecca Lobo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rebecca_Lobo), a former WNBA player, transitioned into a broadcasting career, providing analysis and commentary on WNBA games.

Currently, WNBA games are televised throughout the U.S. by [ABC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_on_ESPN), [ESPN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ESPN), [ESPN2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_on_ESPN), and [Ion Television](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ion_Television) (Ion).

In the early years, two women-oriented networks, [Lifetime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lifetime_\(TV_network\)) and [Oxygen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxygen_\(TV_channel\)), also broadcast games, including the first game of the WNBA. [NBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NBC) showed games from 1997 to 2002 as part of their *[NBA on NBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NBA_on_NBC)* coverage before the league transferred the rights to ABC/ESPN.

In June 2007, the WNBA signed a contract extension with ESPN, the first agreement to pay TV rights fees to a women's professional league. The deal ran from 2009 to 2016. A minimum of 18 games would be broadcast on ABC, ESPN, and [ESPN2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_on_ESPN) each season; the rights to broadcast the first regular-season game and the All-Star game were held by ABC. Also, a minimum of 11 postseason games would be broadcast on any of the three networks.[^cite_note-456] Along with this deal came the first-ever rights fees to be paid to a women's professional sports league. Over the eight years of the contract, "millions and millions of dollars" would be "dispersed to the league's teams".[^cite_note-one-457]

In 2013, the WNBA and ESPN signed a six-year extension on the broadcast deal to cover 2017–2022. In the new deal, a total of 30 games would be shown each season on ESPN networks. Each team would receive around \$1M per year.[^cite_note-458]

On April 22, 2019, [CBS Sports Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CBS_Sports_Network) reached a multi-year deal to televise 40 regular-season weekend and primetime WNBA games, beginning in the [2019 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_WNBA_season).[^cite_note-459][^cite_note-460]

On April 20, 2023, [Ion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ion_Television) signed a multi-year deal with the WNBA to air a 15-week slate of [doubleheader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doubleheader_\(television\)) games on Friday nights (branded as *WNBA Friday Night Spotlight on ION*), beginning with that year's [regular season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_WNBA_season). It was the first national sports broadcast carried by Ion since 2011, and marked the first ever TV contract for [Scripps Sports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scripps_Sports), which was founded by the [E. W. Scripps Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/E._W._Scripps_Company) in December 2022 to acquire sports events for Ion and the group's local TV stations. The agreement also granted local rights to select Ion [O\&Os](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Owned-and-operated_stations) for games involving regional WNBA teams, which stations may carry in early- or late-evening broadcast windows depending on tip-off time.[^cite_note-461][^cite_note-462][^cite_note-463] The first broadcast was on May 26, 2023, when the [Washington Mystics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Mystics) visited the [Chicago Sky](2717051.md).[^cite_note-464] On June 13, 2025, the WNBA announced they had extended their agreement with Scripps Sports to continue the Friday Night Showcase package of games on Ion.[^cite_note-465] On September 30, 2025, the WNBA announced it had also entered an 11-year agreement with [Versant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Versant), the cable spin-off company of [NBCUniversal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NBCUniversal) owned by [Comcast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comcast) shareholders, where its games would air on [USA Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USA_Network).[^cite_note-466][^cite_note-467]

Starting in 2026, the WNBA began a new 11-year agreement in which games returned to NBC Sports, with new airings on USA Sports, as well as remaining on ESPN/ABC and Amazon Prime Video. With this new deal, the league distributes more than 125 games nationally (a minimum of 25 across ESPN/ABC, 50 across NBC platforms, and 30 on Prime Video) and worldwide. ESPN continues as the league's All-Star and Draft broadcaster, Prime Video continues as the broadcaster for the league's Commissioner's Cup games, and NBC picked up rights to all USA Basketball games. In addition, the WNBA Playoffs and Finals will see wider distribution across the three partners, where each partner airs a first round series annually (two on ESPN/ABC and one each on NBC platforms and Prime Video), and rotates turns airing the semifinals and Finals (eight semifinals and five Finals on ESPN/ABC and seven semifinals and three Finals on NBC platforms and Prime Video). The WNBA also stated they expect to add more media partners prior to the first year of the new deal. On March 25, 2026, the WNBA announced the league had reached a new long-term agreement with [CBS Sports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CBS_Sports) to broadcast 20 regular season games on [CBS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CBS) and [Paramount+](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paramount+).[^cite_note-468]

Some teams offer games on local radio, while all teams have some games broadcast on local TV stations:

- **[Atlanta](13930487.md)** – [WANF-TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WANF-TV), [WPCH-TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WPCH-TV) & [Peachtree Sports Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peachtree_Sports_Network)

- **[Chicago](2717051.md)** – [WCIU-TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WCIU-TV)

- **[Dallas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas_Wings)** – [WFAA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WFAA), [KFAA-TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KFAA-TV)

  [^cite_note-469]

  [^cite_note-470]

- **[Phoenix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenix_Mercury)** – [KTVK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KTVK), [KPHE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KPHE)

- **[Seattle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Storm)** – [KOMO-TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KOMO-TV), [KUNS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KUNS)

- **[Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Mystics)** – [Monumental Sports Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monumental_Sports_Network)

### WNBA League Pass

In 2009, the WNBA announced the launch of WNBA LiveAccess, a feature on WNBA.com that provided fans access to over 200 live game webcasts throughout the WNBA season. All WNBA LiveAccess games were then archived for on-demand viewing. Most games (except broadcasts on [ABC, ESPN or ESPN2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_on_ESPN), which are available on [ESPN3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ESPN3)) were available via this system. The first use of LiveAccess was the E League versus [Chicago Sky](2717051.md) preseason game.[^cite_note-472]

Before the 2011 season, LiveAccess was given an overhaul, where the system became more reliable and new features were added. Before the 2012 season, it was announced that users of LiveAccess would have to pay a \$4.99 subscription fee to use the service. In 2013, this was increased to \$14.99. In 2014, the streaming service was renamed [WNBA League Pass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_League_Pass). In 2016, live access was expanded to allow fans to purchase single game or team pass options, as well as a full-season package.[^cite_note-473]

WNBA League Pass is available as part of the WNBA App, the free mobile application available on [iOS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IOS) and [Android](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Android_\(operating_system\)) devices. Games airing on ESPN, ESPN2 as well as other games taking place during the telecast windows of ESPN and ESPN2 games, are not available live on WNBA League Pass. However, those games become available on-demand shortly after the conclusion of their live broadcast.[^cite_note-474]

In 2023, the [Athletes Unlimited](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athletes_Unlimited) (AU) basketball league became available on League Pass. The cost for the subscription was \$24.99 per year.[^cite_note-475] By 2026, the subscription cost \$14.99 monthly or \$39.99 annually, with Amazon being the exclusive distributor of the WNBA League Pass—although it is still available through the WNBA App and website.[^cite_note-476]

- [**<sup>2</sup>**](#ref_Note12) The [Dallas Wings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas_Wings) were known as the [Detroit Shock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_Shock) from 1998 to 2009 and the [Tulsa Shock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulsa_Shock) from 2010 to 2015.

- [**<sup>3</sup>**](#ref_Note13) The [Las Vegas Aces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Las_Vegas_Aces) were known as the [Utah Starzz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utah_Starzz) from 1997 to 2002, the San Antonio Silver Stars from 2003 to 2013, and the [San Antonio Stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Antonio_Stars) from 2014 to 2017.

## Offseason

In July 2023, [Napheesa Collier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napheesa_Collier) and [Breanna Stewart](32433243.md) announced [Unrivaled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unrivaled_\(basketball_league\)), a women's professional three-on-three basketball league.[^cite_note-487] The league was founded, in part, to allow WNBA players to play domestically and to bypass complications from the WNBA's prioritization rule for players who choose to play overseas in the WNBA offseason. Unrivaled's inaugural season, consisting of six teams of six players, debuted in January 2025 in the [Miami](53846.md)-area community of [Medley, Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medley,_Florida).[^cite_note-Yahoo-488]
